Incompatibility of observables, channels and instruments in information theories
Giacomo Mauro D’Ariano, Paolo Perinotti, Alessandro Tosini

Abstract
Every theory of information, including classical and quantum, can be studied in the framework of operational probabilistic theories—where the notion of test generalizes that of quantum instrument, namely a collection of quantum operations summing to a channel, and simple rules are given for the composition of tests in parallel and in sequence. Here we study the notion of compatibility for tests of an operational probabilistic theory. Following the quantum literature, we first introduce the notion of strong compatibility, and then we illustrate its ultimate relaxation, that we deem weak compatibility. It is shown that the two notions coincide in the case of observation tests—which are the counterpart of quantum POVMs—while there exist weakly compatible channels that are not strongly compatible. We prove necessary and sufficient conditions for a theory to exhibit incompatible tests. We show that a theory admits of incompatible tests if and only if some information cannot be extracted without disturbance.



I
Introduction

The existence of incompatible observables, namely measurable quantities that cannot be accessed simultaneously, is at the heart of the departure between classical and quantum physics. Moreover, the presence of incompatible observables stands as a common ground behind several quantum hallmarks, as non-locality
Landau 1987
;
Wolf et al. 2009
;
Oppenheim and Wehner 2010
;
Coecke et al. 2012
;
Khrennikov 2019
and contextuality
Kochen and Specker 1967
;
Fine 1982
;
Spekkens 2005
;
Cabello et al. 2014
;
Acín et al. 2015
;
Bermejo-Vega et al. 2017
;
Dzhafarov and Kujala 2017
;
Abramsky et al. 2017
, and quantum no-go theorems, such as no-cloning
Wootters and Zurek 1982
and no-information without disturbance
Busch 2009
.

All the above effects related to quantum incompatibility have been studied in the details both within the mathematical
framework of quantum theory and from a more fundamental perspective as features of an arbitrary information theory.
The same fundamental analysis can be carried out for the primitive notion of incompatibility, a program already
started within quantum logics
Lahti 1980
, and carried on in the last decade by Heinosaari and
coauthors
Heinosaari et al. 2016
;
Heinosaari and Miyadera 2017
. In the latter works, an operational definition
of compatibility for
quantum observables
and
quantum channels
is given and thoroughly characterized.
According to this approach two observables (channels) are compatible if there exists another observable (channel) that
jointly implements them.

In this paper we expand the operational notion of compatible processes for an arbitrary theory of information, and
deepen its analysis; to this end we consider the framework of
operational probabilistic theories

Chiribella et al. 2010
;
D’Ariano et al. 2017
. Moreover, we look at the compatibility of two arbitrary
devices, what in quantum theory are called
quantum instruments
or more generally
tests
in an operational
probabilistic theory. The compatibility of observables and channels, which are two kinds of tests, will be included in
this analysis as special cases.

Given two devices, say A and B, two inequivalent operational notions of compatibility between them can be introduced.
In the first case one one can say that A and B are compatible if there exists a third device, say C, that jointly
implements both A and B. This is the spirit of references
Heinosaari et al. 2016
;
Heinosaari and Miyadera 2017

on compatibility of quantum observables and channels. Alternatively, as proposed in this work, one can say that A and
B are compatible if there exists a third device, say C, that after realizing A, leaves the possibility open of
converting it to B via a post-processing, and viceversa there exists another device, say D, that first realizes B and
then A via post-processing. We formalize the above intuitive ideas into two notions of compatibility, here denoted

strong
and
weak compatibility
, for tests of an operational probabilistic theory. As suggested by the
names, compatible tests according to the first notion are also compatible according to the second one, while the
converse is not true. While the two conditions coincide for observation tests, they indeed differ for general tests;
e.g. in quantum theory every pair of channels is weakly compatible, while there are pairs that are not strongly
compatible.

The fundamental question, once compatibility is thoroughly defined, is to what other features of a theory is
it related, or in technical terms what are necessary or sufficient conditions for a theory to exhibit incompatible
tests. Answering this question allows one to understand quantum incompatibility in relation to other operational
aspects of the theory, rather than observing it as a sheer consequence of its mathematical formulation. Here we show
that compatibility of tests is intimately related to the possibility of measuring any conceivable quantity without
introducing disturbance on the measured system, and in turn this is equivalent to the request that all systems of the
theory are classical.

After briefly reviewing the framework of operational probabilistic theories in Section
II
, in Section
III
we define strong and weak compatibility of tests. In Section
IV
we prove necessary and sufficient conditions for a theory that does not contain any weakly incompatible tests, here denoted
fully compatible theory
. Among those conditions a theory is fully compatible if and only if it satisfies
full information without disturbance
, namely all the information encoded in the states of the theory can be read without disturbance.

II
Operational probabilistic theories

The framework in which we introduce and study the notion of compatibile devices is that of
operational
probabilistic theories
(OPT). Here we provide a brief review of the framework, with focus on those aspects that
will be used in the remainder. For details and proofs, we refer
to
Chiribella et al. 2010
;
D’Ariano et al. 2017

The essential building blocks of an operational theory are

tests
,
events
, and
systems
. A test

\mathsf{A}=\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
is the collection of events
\mathcal{A}_{i}
, where
i

labels the element of the outcome space
{\rm X}
of the test. Tests generalize the notion are the of quantum
instruments, i.e. collections of quantum operations that sum to a channel. An input and an output
system
are
associated to any test (event) with the following diagrammatic representation

One can compose tests in sequence and in parallel.

given two tests
\mathsf{A}=\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
and
\mathsf{B}=\{\mathcal{B}_{j}\}_{j\in{\rm Y}}
such that the input system of
\mathsf{B}
coincides with the output system of
\mathsf{A}
we can define their sequential composition as the test given by the collection of events

deterministic
event.
For every system
{\rm A}
there exists a unique singleton test

\{\mathcal{I}_{{\rm A}}\}
such that
\mathcal{I}_{{\rm B}}\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}\mathcal{I}_{{\rm A}}=\mathcal{A}
for every

\mathcal{A}

and output
{\rm B}

\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}

identity
of system
{\rm A}
.

for every couple of systems

C}}}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 40.00006pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-6.99997pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 10.00002pt\raise-6.99997pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-6.04515pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{{}^{{\rm B}}_{\phantom{{\rm B}}}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt\raise-1.98611pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{B}_{j}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.00005pt\raise-6.99997pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-6.26042pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{{}^{{\rm D}}_{\phantom{{\rm D}}}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 40.00006pt\raise-6.99997pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{aligned}\,,

and satisfying the condition

(\mathcal{C}_{h}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{k})(\mathcal{A}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{j})=(\mathcal{C}_{h}\mathcal{A}_{i})\otimes(\mathcal{D}_{k}\mathcal{B}_{j}).

In quantum theory the parallel composition is the usual tensor product of
linear maps. However, for a general OPT, the parallel composition may
not coincide with a tensor product. We keep the notation
\otimes
for an arbitrary parallel composition rule.
Intuitively speaking, the parallel composition of two tests represents their independent action on the two subsystems
of a composite system.

There exists a
trivial system

{\rm I}
such that
{\rm A}{\rm I}={\rm I}{\rm A}={\rm A}
for every system
{\rm A}
. Tests with
input system
{\rm I}
and output
{\rm A}
are called
preparation tests

of
{\rm A}
, with events denoted as boxes without the input wire

\textstyle{{{}^{{\rm A}}_{\phantom{{\rm A}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

(or in formula as round kets
\left|\rho\right)_{\rm A}
), while the tests with input system
{\rm A}
and output
{\rm I}
are
called
observation tests
of
{\rm A}
, made of events with no output wire

\textstyle{{{}^{{\rm A}}_{\phantom{{\rm A}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

(in formula round bras
\left(c\right|_{\rm A}
). We will use the Greek letters for preparation events,
e.g.
\mathsf{P}=\{\rho_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
and Latin letters for observation events, e.g.
\mathsf{C}=\{c_{j}\}_{j\in{\rm X}}
.

In summary, an operational theory is a collection of systems and tests, closed under parallel and sequential
composition, with a trivial system, and an identity test for every system of the theory.

Via sequential and parallel composition of diagrams one can build up
circuits
that correspond
to arbitrary tests. A circuit is
closed
if it starts with a
preparation test and ends with an observation test, namely its overall input and output systems are trivial. An

operational probabilistic theory
(OPT) is an operational
theory where any closed circuit of tests corresponds to a
probability distribution for the joint test. Compound tests from the
trivial system to itself are independent, both for sequential and
parallel composition, namely their joint probability distribution is
given by the product of the component joint probability
distributions. For example the application of an observation event

c_{i}
after the preparation event
\rho_{j}
corresponds to the closed
circuit
\left(c_{i}\right|\left.\!\rho_{j}\right)_{\rm A}
and denotes the probability of
the outcome
(i,j)
of the observation test
\mathsf{c}
after the
preparation test
\mathsf{\rho}
of system
{\rm A}
, i.e.

Given a system
{\rm A}
of a probabilistic theory we can quotient the set
of preparation events of
{\rm A}
by the equivalence relation

\left|\rho\right)_{\rm A}\sim\left|\sigma\right)_{\rm A}\Leftrightarrow\left(c\right|\left.\!\rho\right)_{\rm A}=\left(c\right|\left.\!\sigma\right)_{\rm A}

for every observation event
c
. Similarly, we can quotient
observation events. The equivalence classes of preparation events and
observation events of
{\rm A}
will be denoted by the same symbols as
their elements
\left|\rho\right)_{\rm A}
and
\left(c\right|_{\rm A}
, respectively, and will
be called
state
and
effect
for system
{\rm A}
. We will denote by
{\mathsf{St}}({\rm A})
,
{\mathsf{Eff}}({\rm A})

the sets of states and effects of system
{\rm A}
. States and effects
are real-valued functionals on each other, and can be
naturally embedded in reciprocally dual real vector spaces,

{\mathsf{St}}_{\mathbb{R}}({\rm A})
and
{\mathsf{Eff}}_{\mathbb{R}}({\rm A})
, whose
dimension is assumed to be finite. An event
\mathcal{A}
with input system
{\rm A}
and output system
{\rm B}
induces a
linear map from
{\mathsf{St}}_{\mathbb{R}}({\rm A}{\rm C})
to

{\mathsf{St}}_{\mathbb{R}}({\rm B}{\rm C})
for each ancillary system
{\rm C}
. The
collection of all these maps (for each system
{\rm C}
) is a
transformation
from

{\rm A}
to
{\rm B}
, that represents the event
\mathcal{A}
. In the following, the symbols
\mathcal{A}
and

\textstyle{{{}^{{\rm A}}_{\phantom{{\rm A}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{}^{{\rm B}}_{\phantom{{\rm B}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

will be used to represent the transformation corresponding to
the event
\mathcal{A}
. We will denote by
{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A},{\rm B})
, with linear
span
{\mathsf{Transf}}_{\mathbb{R}}({\rm A},{\rm B})
, the set of transformations from
{\rm A}
to
{\rm B}
. A linear map

\mathcal{A}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}_{\mathbb{R}}({\rm A},{\rm B})
is
admissible
if it
locally preserves the set of states
{\mathsf{St}}({\rm A}{\rm C})
, namely

\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{{\rm C}}({\mathsf{St}}({\rm A}{\rm C}))\subseteq{\mathsf{St}}({\rm B}{\rm C})
, for
every
{\rm C}
. Given two maps
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B})}
, one has

\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, if and only if

64.17487pt\raise 4.49997pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hbox{\cirbuild@}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 64.17487pt\raise-15.99991pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hbox{\cirbuild@}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-8.8889pt\raise-11.49994pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 5.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{\Psi}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 18.88892pt\raise-11.49994pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 28.88893pt\raise-11.49994pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-6.10417pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{{}^{{\rm C}}_{\phantom{{\rm 38.88895pt\raise-11.49994pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 48.88896pt\raise-11.49994pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 5.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{a}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{aligned}\ ,

for every
{\rm C}
, every
\Psi\in{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A}{\rm C})}
, and every

a\in{\mathsf{Eff}}({\rm B}{\rm C})
, namely they give the same probabilities within
every possible closed circuit.

A deterministic transformation (i.e. a transformation with conditional probability equal to 1 in every circuit) is
also called a
channel
. A transformation
\mathcal{A}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A}\to{\rm B})
is called
reversible
if there
exists a transformation
\mathcal{B}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm B}\to{\rm A})
such that
\mathcal{B}\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}
and
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{I}_{\rm B}
. In this case the
inverse
\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{A}
is denoted as
\mathcal{A}^{-1}
. A related notion is that of a
left-reversible
transformation,
that is a transformation
\mathcal{A}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A}\to{\rm B})
for which there exists
\mathcal{B}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm B}\to{\rm A})

such that
\mathcal{B}\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}
. In this case we say that
\mathcal{B}
is a left-inverse of
\mathcal{A}
. In turn,
\mathcal{B}
is

right-reversible
, and
\mathcal{A}
is a right-inverse of
\mathcal{B}
. Notice that, unlike the inverse of a reversible
transformation, the left-inverse as well as the right-inverse might not be unique.

We finally introduce the notions of
refinable
and
atomic
event.

Definition 1

(Refinement and coarse-graining)
.

A
refinement
of an event

\mathcal{A}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A},{\rm B})
is a collection of events
\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
from
{\rm A}
to
{\rm B}
, such that there exists a
test
\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm Y}}
with
X\subseteq Y
and

\mathcal{A}=\sum_{i\in{\rm X}}\mathcal{B}_{i}
. Conversely,
\mathcal{A}
is called the
coarse-graining
of the events
\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
.
Accordingly, a test
\mathsf{D}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{D}_{l}\}_{l\in{\rm W}}
is a
refinement
of the test
\mathsf{C}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{C}_{k}\}_{k\in{\rm Z}}

if there exists a partition of
{\rm W}
into disjoint sets
{\rm W}_{k}
such that
\mathcal{C}_{k}=\sum_{l\in{\rm W}_{k}}\mathcal{D}_{l}
for every
k\in{\rm Z}
; conversely, we say that
\mathsf{C}
is a
coarse-graining
of
\mathsf{D}
.

For simplicity we will also refer to a refinement of
\mathcal{A}
as
\mathcal{A}=\sum_{i\in{\rm X}}\mathcal{B}_{i}
, without specifying the test including the events
\mathcal{B}_{i}
.

Definition 2

(Refinable and atomic events)
.

Given two events
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A},{\rm B})
we say that
\mathcal{B}

refines

\mathcal{A}
if there exist a refinement

\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
of
\mathcal{A}
such that
\mathcal{B}\in\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
. A refinement

\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
of
\mathcal{A}
is
trivial
if
\mathcal{B}_{i}=\lambda_{i}\mathcal{A}
,

\lambda_{i}\in[0,1]
, for every
i\in{\rm X}
. An event
\mathcal{A}
is

atomic
if it admits only of trivial refinements. An event is refinable
if it is not atomic.

As in quantum theory, the word
pure
is used as
synonym of atomic in the special case of states, and as usual a state that is not pure will be called
mixed
.

A test where the same event, scaled with some probability, occurs more than once is redundant: indeed, it corresponds
to a test where, upon occurrence of the outcome corresponding to the given event, a coin is flipped or some other
random variable is sampled, and the outcome is supplemented with the value of the random variable. It is clear that
the supplemental information in this case has nothing to do with the system undergoing the test. It is then often
convenient to consider tests where redundancy is removed:

Definition 3

(Non redundant test)
.

We call a test
\mathsf{A}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}

non redundant
when for every pair
i,j\in{\rm X}
one has

\mathcal{A}_{i}\neq\lambda\mathcal{A}_{j}
for
\lambda>0
.

From a redundant test one can always achieve a ”maximal non redundant” one by taking the test made of coarse grainings
of all the sets of proportional elements.

II.1
Causality and post-processing

A notion at the basis of compatibility between tests will be that of
post-processing
, here introduced for
operational probabilistic theories in analogy to the post-processing of quantum
instruments
Leppäjärvi and Sedlák 2021
. The post-processing of a test
\mathsf{A}
, roughly speaking, corresponds to a
second test that is run at the output of the test
\mathsf{A}
. As one expects, the most general post-processing of a
test exploits the possibility of using its outcomes for conditioning the choice of the post-processing maps. However,
not every OPT offers the possibility to post-process tests in an arbitrary way. For this reason, throughout the paper
we will restrict to causal theories
Perinotti 2020
, where using outcomes to condition the
choice of a subsequent test always leads to admissible tests.

Definition 4

(Strongly causal theories)
.

A theory is
strongly causal
if for every test
\mathsf{A}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B})}
and every collection of tests
\mathsf{P}^{(i)}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{P}^{(i)}_{j}\}_{j\in{\rm Y}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm B},{\rm C})}
, labelled by the outcomes
i\in{\rm X}
of test
\mathsf{A}
, the test
\mathsf{B}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{B}_{i,j}\}_{(i,j)\in{\rm X}\times{\rm Y}}\subseteq{{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm C})}}
, with events

,

is a test of the theory.

We remark that, as suggested by the name, this notion of causality is strictly stronger than the uniqueness of the
deterministic effect
Chiribella et al. 2010
, that is often used as a definition of causality, and here we will call

weak causality
for the sake of clarity.

Proposition 1

(Unique deterministic effect
Chiribella et al. 2010
)
.

In a strongly causal theory, for every
system there exists a unique deterministic effect.

For the remainder we will assume strong causality, unless otherwise stated. Accordingly, in the following we will
assume that there exists a unique way to discard a system, the counterpart of the trace operator in quantum theory.
Therefore, given a test

\mathsf{A}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B})}
there exists a unique observation-test associated
to it, which is the test
\mathsf{a}\coloneqq\{a_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm A})}
with events
\left(a_{i}\right|=\left(e\right|\mathcal{A}_{i}
,
and
\left(e\right|
the deterministic effect of system
{\rm B}
. We remind that in a (weakly) causal theory a transformation

\mathcal{C}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B})}
is a channel if and only if
\left(e\right|\mathcal{C}=\left(e\right|
. Moreover, one can always safely extend a
strongly causal theory so that one can always assume that every state is proportional to a deterministic one,
including in the theory every state

\rho\in{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A})}
such that
\left(e\right|\left.\!\rho\right)=1

Perinotti 2020
.

In a (weakly) causal theory, the uniqueness of the deterministic effect clearly introduces an asymmetry between input
and output of a test or a transformation. In this context it is then reasonable to define a reversible dilation
of a test
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A}\to{\rm B})
as follows: we say that
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
has a

left-reversible dilation
if there exist a system
\tilde{\rm B}
, a left-reversible transformation

\mathcal{V}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A}\to{\rm B}\tilde{\rm B})
, and an observation-test

\{a_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Eff}}(\tilde{\rm B})
, such that for every
i\in{\rm X}
one has

In the special case of a singleton test
\{\mathcal{A}\}
, the above definition becomes that of a reversible dilation
of channel
\mathcal{A}
. Spelling out the definition, we see that
\mathcal{A}
has a left-reversible dilation if
there exist a system
\tilde{\rm B}
and a left-reversible transformation

\mathcal{V}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A}\to{\rm B}\tilde{\rm B})
, such that

A related, but stronger notion is the following: we say that
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
has a

reversible dilation
if there exist systems
\tilde{\rm A}
and
\tilde{\rm B}
, a reversible transformation

\mathcal{U}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A}\tilde{\rm A}\to{\rm B}\tilde{\rm B})
, a state
\eta\in{\mathsf{St}}(\tilde{\rm A})
, and an observation-test

\{a_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Eff}}(\tilde{\rm B})
, such that for every
i\in{\rm X}

Notice that a reversible dilation
(\tilde{\rm A},\tilde{\rm B},\eta,\mathcal{U},\{a_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}})
of an instrument

\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
straightforwardly allows one to define a left-reversible one

(\tilde{\rm B},\mathcal{V},\{a_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}})
, just taking

(2)

As anticipated, we can now introduce the notion of test post-processing, that is generally a conditioned test,
possibly followed by a coarse-graining and/or the discarding of an ancillary system.

Definition 5
.

(Test post-processing)
The test
\mathsf{C}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{C}_{k}\}_{k\in{\rm Z}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm C})}
is a
post-processing
of the test

\mathsf{A}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B})}
if there exists a collection of tests

\mathsf{P}^{(i)}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{P}^{(i)}_{j}\}_{j\in{\rm Y}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm B},{\rm C}{\rm C}^{\prime})}
,
i\in{\rm X}
and a partition of
{\rm X}\times{\rm Y}
into disjoint sets
{\rm W}_{k}
such that

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise We stress that assuming only the framework of operational probabilistic theories, without further requirements, a post-processing of a test would not necessarily correspond to a test of the theory, while for strongly causal theories any post-processing is admissible, since the conditioned tests
\mathsf{B}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{B}_{i,j}\}_{(i,j)\in{\rm X}\times{\rm Y}}\subseteq{{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm C}{\rm C}^{\prime})}}
in Eq. (
5
) is a test of the theory.

III
Compatible tests

We now introduce a first operational notion of compatibility of tests, that we deem strong compatibility, based on
the idea that two tests are compatible if there exists a procedure, namely another test, for their joint realization.
Strong compatibility extends the compatibility of quantum channels studied in Refs.
Heinosaari et al. 2016
;
Heinosaari and Miyadera 2017
to tests of an arbitrary theory.

Definition 6

(Strong compatibility)
.

We say that the tests
\mathsf{A}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B})}
and
\mathsf{B}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{B}_{j}\}_{j\in{\rm Y}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm C})}
are
strongly compatible
, and we write
\mathsf{A}\Join\mathsf{B}
, if there exists a test
\mathsf{C}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{C}_{k}\}_{k\in{\rm Z}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B}{\rm C})}
such that for every
i\in{\rm X}
and
j\in{\rm Y}
one has

for two partitions of
{\rm Z}
into disjoint subsets
{\rm V}_{i}
,
i\in{\rm X}
and
{\rm W}_{j}
,
j\in{\rm Y}
, respectively.

The following proposition shows that, without loss of generality, the test that jointly realizes two strongly
compatible tests can be chosen as a double-outcome test, whose outcomes are pairs with elements corresponding
respectively to the outcomes of the compatible tests.

Proposition 2

(Strong compatibility)
.

Two tests
\mathsf{A}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B})}
and
\mathsf{B}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{B}_{j}\}_{j\in{\rm Y}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm C})}

Proof.

Since
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
are strongly compatible then according to Definition
6
, Eqs. (
5
) and (
5
) hold for a test
\mathsf{C}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{C}_{k}\}_{k\in{\rm Z}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B}{\rm C})}
.
Consider now the disjoint partition of
{\rm Z}
given by
\cup_{i,j}{\rm U}_{ij}={\rm Z}
, with
{\rm U}_{ij}={\rm V}_{i}\cap{\rm W}_{j}
, and define the test

\mathsf{D}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{D}_{i,j}\}_{(i,j)\in{\rm X}\times{\rm Y}}\subseteq{({\rm A},{\rm B}{\rm C})}
, with

\mathcal{D}_{i,j}=\sum_{k\in{\rm V}_{i}\cap{\rm W}_{j}}\mathcal{C}_{k}.

It is straightforward to see that
\mathsf{D}
satisfies Eqs. (
6
) and (
6
).
The converse implication is trivial.

\,\blacksquare

Another operational notion of compatibility of tests, that we will show to be strictly weaker than the previous one, is proposed in the following. We say two tests
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
are compatible if there exists a realization of
\mathsf{A}
that can be post-processed to get
\mathsf{B}
, and there exists a realization of
\mathsf{B}
that can be post-processed to get
\mathsf{A}
. In other words it is possible to realize one of the two tests leaving open the possibility of deciding later to make the other one.

Definition 7
.

We say that the test
\mathsf{A}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B})}

does not exclude
the
test
\mathsf{B}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{B}_{j}\}_{j\in{\rm Y}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm C})}
, and write
\mathsf{A}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}
, if
one has

for some test
\mathsf{C}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{C}_{k}\}_{k\in{\rm Z}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B}{\rm B}^{\prime})}
, post-processing tests

\mathsf{P}^{(k)}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{P}_{l}^{(k)}\}_{l\in{\rm L}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm B}{\rm B}^{\prime},{\rm C}{\rm C}^{\prime})}
, and disjoint partions
{\rm V}_{i},i\in{\rm X}
and
{\rm W}_{j},j\in{\rm Y}
of
{\rm Z}
and
{\rm Z}\times{\rm L}
respectively.

The above notion allows us now to define weak compatibility as follows.

Definition 8

(Weak compatibility)
.

We say that
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
are
weakly-compatible
, and write
\mathsf{A}\leftrightarrow\mathsf{B}
, if

\mathsf{A}
does not exclude
\mathsf{B}
, and
\mathsf{B}
does not exclude
\mathsf{A}
.

Notice that the condition
\mathsf{A}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}
does not imply
\mathsf{B}\rightarrow\mathsf{A}
. Moreover, given
two weakly compatible tests
\mathsf{A}\leftrightarrow\mathsf{B}
, the test
\mathsf{C}
used for realizing
\mathsf{A}
, and
subsequently post-processing it to
\mathsf{B}
(
\mathsf{A}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}
), may be different from the test

\mathsf{C}^{\prime}
used to first realize
\mathsf{B}
and then
\mathsf{A}
(
\mathsf{B}\rightarrow\mathsf{A}
). This is in
the spirit of keeping the present notion of compatibility condition the weakest possible.

III.1
Comparing strong and weak compatibility

In this section we compare the two definitions of compatibility provided so far. First we prove that strong-compatibility always implies weak-compatibility.

Proposition 3
.

Given two tests
\mathsf{A}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B})}
and
\mathsf{B}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{B}_{j}\}_{j\in{\rm Y}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm C})}
, if they are strongly compatible then they are also weakly compatible.

Proof.

By hypothesis
\mathsf{A}\Join\mathsf{B}
. According to Definition
6
there exists a test
\mathsf{C}\coloneqq\{C_{k}\}_{k\in{\rm Z}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B}{\rm C})}
for the joint realization of
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
as in Eqs. (
5
) and (
5
), respectively. Now we prove that it is also
\mathsf{A}\leftrightarrow{B}
. Let us start with
\mathsf{A}\rightarrow{B}
and show that Eqs. (
7
) and (
7
) in Definition
7
can be satisfied. First one can rewrite Eqs. (
5
) and (
5
) as follows

where
\mathcal{S}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm B}{\rm C},{\rm B}{\rm C})}
is the deterministic transformation swapping systems
{\rm B}
and
{\rm C}
. Then we observe that the two equations above also give instances of Eqs. (
7
) and (
7
). The first equation coincides with Eq. (
7
) upon identyfing system
{\rm B}^{\prime}
with system
{\rm C}
and system
{\rm C}^{\prime}
with system
{\rm B}
. The second is a special case of Eq. (
7
) where the post-processing tests
\mathsf{P}^{(k)}
are all chosen equal to the singleton test made of the swap gate
\mathcal{S}
. Accordingly, one has
{\rm Z}\times{\rm L}={\rm Z}
, since
{\rm L}
is trivial, and the disjoint partition
{\rm W}_{j},j\in{\rm Y}
of
{\rm Z}
, is thus a disjoint partition of
{\rm Z}\times{\rm L}
as well.
Analogously, one can verify that
\mathsf{B}\rightarrow\mathsf{A}
holds.

\,\blacksquare

We continue the comparison in the special cases of observation tests, say tests having classical outcomes, and for channels, say tests having non trivial output system but only one possible outcome.

III.1.1
The case of observables

Using the definitions presented in this section one can study compatibility between observation tests as a special case. In quantum theory observation tests are characterized by having a nontrivial input system, but a trivial output one, thus only producing a classical outcome. This case is different from instruments, where both the input and the output are non-trivial, and thus one has both a quantum output and a classical outcome. Also for an arbitrary theory observation tests only produce classical outcomes. The scope of this section is to derive the consequences of this simplification on the relation between strong and weak compatibility for observation tests.

The following proposition shows the collapse of the two notions of compatibility for two observation tests, and the
equivalence of both conditions with the the possibility of detecting simultaneously the outcomes of the two tests.

Proposition 4

(Compatible observation tests)
.

For two observation tests
\mathsf{A}\coloneqq\{a_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\in{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm A})}
and

\mathsf{B}\coloneqq\{b_{j}\}_{j\in{\rm Y}}\in{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm A})}
the following statements are equivalent

1.

\mathsf{A}\Join\mathsf{B}
, namely
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
are strongly-compatible

2.

\mathsf{A}\leftrightarrow\mathsf{B}
, namely
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
are weakly-compatible

3.

\mathsf{A}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}
(or
\mathsf{B}\rightarrow\mathsf{A}
)

4.

There exists an oservation test
\mathsf{C}\coloneqq\{c_{i,j}\}_{(i,j)\in{\rm X}\times{\rm Y}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm A})}
such that

(9)

Proof.

We show the equivalence proving the chain of implications
\ref{obs:1}\Rightarrow\ref{obs:2}\Rightarrow\ref{obs:3}\Rightarrow\ref{obs:4}\Rightarrow\ref{obs:1}
.
The implication
\ref{obs:1}\Rightarrow\ref{obs:2}
follows from Proposition
3
.
The implication
\ref{obs:2}\Rightarrow\ref{obs:3}
is trivial, since
\mathsf{A}\leftrightarrow\mathsf{B}
if and only if it is both
\mathsf{A}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}
and
\mathsf{B}\rightarrow\mathsf{A}
.
Consider now the implication
\ref{obs:3}\Rightarrow\ref{obs:4}
. If
\mathsf{A}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}
then
by Definition
7
there exist a test
\mathsf{D}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{D}_{k}\}_{k\in{\rm Z}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A}\to{\rm B})}

and for every
k\in{\rm Z}
a post-processing test

\mathsf{P}^{(k)}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{P}_{l}^{(k)}\}_{l\in{\rm L}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm B}\to{\rm C})}
such that

\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{{}^{{\rm A}}_{\phantom{{\rm A}}}}}

where the first equality follows from Eq. (
10
) reminding that
\sum_{l}\mathcal{P}^{(k)}_{l}
are channels and then
preserve the deterministic effect. Thus we have two different coarse-grainings of the same test
\mathsf{F}
, given by

\sum_{(k,l)\in{\rm V}_{i}\times{\rm L}}\left(f_{k,l}\right|=\left(a_{i}\right|
and
\sum_{(k,l)\in{\rm W}_{j}}\left(f_{k,l}\right|=\left(b_{j}\right|
, corresponding to
the two observation tests
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
, respectively. We can now simply define the observation test

\mathsf{C}\coloneqq\{c_{i,j}\}_{(i,j)\in{\rm X}\otimes{\rm Y}}\in{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm A})}
with

\left(c_{i,j}\right|=\sum_{(k,l)\in({\rm V}_{i}\times{\rm L})\cap{\rm W}_{j}}\left(f_{k,l}\right|
such that
4
is satisfied. This proves that

\mathsf{A}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}
implies
4
and exactly the same argument works assuming

\mathsf{B}\rightarrow\mathsf{A}
in place of of
\mathsf{A}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}
.
We conclude the proof showing that
4
\Rightarrow
1
. In the case of interest the condition in
Eq. (
6
) simplifies, being systems
{\rm B}
and
{\rm C}
trivial. Thus, the condition boils down to
having an observation test
\{c_{i,j}\}_{(i,j)\in{\rm X}\times{\rm Y}}
whose marginal coarse-grainings in
Eq. (
6
) are equal to
a_{i}
and
b_{j}
, respectively. However, this is precisely the hypothesis in
item
4
.

\,\blacksquare

As a final remark in this subsection, we notice that two observation tests are compatible if and only if they are the measurements associated to two compatible instruments, both in the weak and in the strong sense:

Proposition 5
.

The four conditions in Proposition
4
are also equivalent to the following two

5.

There exist two tests
\tilde{\mathsf{A}}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B})}
and
\tilde{\mathsf{B}}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{B}_{j}\}_{j\in{\rm Y}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm C})}
, with
\left(a_{i}\right|\coloneqq\left(e\right|\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
\left(b_{j}\right|\coloneqq\left(e\right|\mathcal{B}_{j}
such that
\tilde{\mathsf{A}}\Join\tilde{\mathsf{B}}
;

6.

There exist two tests
\tilde{\mathsf{A}}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B})}
and
\tilde{\mathsf{B}}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{B}_{j}\}_{j\in{\rm Y}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm C})}
, with
\left(a_{i}\right|\coloneqq\left(e\right|\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
\left(b_{j}\right|\coloneqq\left(e\right|\mathcal{B}_{j}
such that
\tilde{\mathsf{A}}\leftrightarrow\tilde{\mathsf{B}}
.

Proof.

The scheme of the proof is the following:
\ref{it:strongtest}\Rightarrow\ref{it:weaktest}\Rightarrow\ref{obs:1}\Leftrightarrow\ref{obs:4}\Rightarrow\ref{it:strongtest}
.
By Proposition
3
, clearly
\ref{it:strongtest}\Rightarrow\ref{it:weaktest}
. Now,
one can straightforwardly see that
\ref{it:weaktest}\Rightarrow\ref{obs:1}
, taking the definition of weak compatibility for
\tilde{\mathsf{A}}
and
\tilde{\mathsf{B}}
, and discarding both outputs. Finally, one can prove that
\ref{obs:4}\Rightarrow\ref{it:strongtest}
as follows. Define the tests
\tilde{\mathsf{A}}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B})}
,

\tilde{\mathsf{B}}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{B}_{j}\}_{j\in{\rm Y}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm C})}
, and
\tilde{\mathsf{C}}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{C}_{i,j}\}_{(i,j)\in{\rm X}\times{\rm Y}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B}{\rm C})}
as follows

where
\{\omega\}\subseteq{\mathsf{St}}({\rm B})
, and
\{\nu\}\subseteq{\mathsf{St}}({\rm C})
are two arbitrary deterministic preparation tests. It immediately follows that
\left(a_{i}\right|=\left(e\right|\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
\left(b_{j}\right|=\left(e\right|\mathcal{B}_{j}
. Using
\tilde{\mathsf{C}}
for the joint simulation of
\tilde{\mathsf{A}}
and
\tilde{\mathsf{B}}
, one then has
\tilde{\mathsf{A}}\Join\tilde{\mathsf{B}}
.

\,\blacksquare

III.1.2
The case of channels

Also the compatibility between channels, i.e. deterministic tests made of a single event, is a special case of
the one given for general tests. However, in this case strong and weak compatibility do not lead to the same notion as it was for observables.
For the convenience of the reader we rewrite Definitions
6
and
8
in the case of channels.

According to Definition
6
two channels
\mathcal{A}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B})}
and
\mathcal{B}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm C})}
are strongly compatible if there exists a channel
\mathcal{C}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B}{\rm C})}
such that

for some test
\mathsf{C}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{C}_{k}\}_{k\in{\rm Z}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B}{\rm B}^{\prime})}
and deterministic transformations
\mathcal{P}^{(k)}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm B}{\rm B}^{\prime},{\rm C}{\rm C}^{\prime})}
(and analogous condition for
\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
).
The non-equivalence between the two conditions above is a consequence of the following lemmas, the first already proved in Ref.
Heinosaari and Miyadera 2017
.

Lemma 1

(Broadcasting via strongly-compatible reversible channels)
.

If there exist two left-reversible channels
\mathcal{A}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B})}
and
\mathcal{B}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm C})}
that are strongly compatible then there exists a broadcasting map for system
{\rm A}
.

Proof.

A broadcasting map for system
{\rm A}
is a transformation
\mathcal{C}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A},{\rm A}{\rm A})
such that, for every state
\rho\in{\mathsf{St}}({\rm A}{\rm D})
, the state
\mathcal{C}\left|\rho\right)\in{\mathsf{St}}({\rm A}{\rm A}{\rm D})
has both marginals on system
{\rm A}
equal to
\rho
. Suppose now that
\mathcal{A}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B})}
and
\mathcal{B}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm C})}
are left-reversible and strongly-compatible, namely there exists a channel
\mathcal{C}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A},{\rm B}{\rm C})
with marginals equal to
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
(see Eqs. (
17
) and (
17
)). The channel

\mathcal{W}
such that
\mathcal{V}\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}
and
\mathcal{W}\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}
respectively, has both marginals equal to the identity channel, thus providing a broadcasting channel.

\,\blacksquare

Lemma 2

(Weak-compatibility of channels with reversible dilation)
.

Two channels with reversible dilation are weakly-compatible.

Proof.

Consider two channels
\mathcal{A}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B})}
and
\mathcal{B}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm C})}
, and suppose that they have a left-
reversible dilation, namely there exist reversible transformations
\mathcal{V}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B}\tilde{\rm B})}
and
\mathcal{W}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm C}\tilde{\rm C})}
, such that

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise \displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{e}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.65627pt\raise 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{e}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.65627pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{e}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{aligned}\,.

Now it is immediate to see that
\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
: indeed one can take
\mathcal{V}
in place of test
\mathsf{C}
in Eq. (
18
) and
\mathcal{W}\tilde{\mathcal{V}}
as post-processing in Eq.
18
, where
\tilde{\mathcal{V}}
is a left-inverse of
\mathcal{V}
. Analogously, one can check that
\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
using first
\mathcal{W}
to realize
\mathcal{B}
, by discarding system
\tilde{\rm C}
and
\mathcal{V}\tilde{\mathcal{W}}
, with
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}
a left-inverse of
\mathcal{W}
, as post-processing to get
\mathcal{A}
after discarding system
\tilde{\rm B}
.

\,\blacksquare

Corollary 1
.

Strong compatibility is strictly stronger than weak compatibility.

Proof.

This follows from the case of quantum channels. Since quantum theory satisfies no-broadcasting
Barnum et al. 1996
, no pair of reversible channels can be strongly compatible, according to Lemma
1
. On the other hand, quantum theory allows for reversible dilation of channels and thus, due to Lemma
2
, every pair of channels is weakly-compatible in quantum theory.

\,\blacksquare

IV
Theories with no incompatible tests

In this section we characterize theories that do not exhibit any pair of incompatible tests in the weak (and then also in the strong) sense. We call this feature full compatibility and show necessary and sufficient conditions for it. Among them is the equivalence between the class of operational probabilistic theories with full compatibility and the class of strongly causal theories with full information without disturbance, namely theories where all information can be extracted without introducing disturbance. This last property has been presented in Ref.
D’Ariano et al. 2020a
, where the authors prove criteria for studying the information-disturbance trade-off in an arbitrary operational probabilistic theory. A brief account of the results in Ref.
D’Ariano et al. 2020a
is given in the next section.

IV.1
Information-disturbance relation

Probabilistic theories can be divided in three classes according their information-disturbance trade-off:

(i)

No-information without disturbance
: this class encompasses theories where any test that provides
information must disturb. This is the case e.g. of quantum
theory
Busch et al. 2014
;
Busch 2009
;
D’Ariano et al. 2017
, or non-local boxes, as proved in
Ref.
D’Ariano et al. 2020a
.

(ii)

Information without disturbance
: this is the class corresponding to the most typical scenario, where some
information can be extracted without disturbance and some other information cannot.

(iii)

Full-information without disturbance
: this is the class of theories where all information can be extracted
without disturbance. The prototype of theories in this class is classical information theory.

This classification is based on the following definition of disturbance that does not rely on any specific feature of the theory.

Definition 9

(Non-disturbing test)
.

A test
\mathsf{A}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B})}
is
non-disturbing

if
\sum_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}=\mathcal{R}
, where
\mathcal{R}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B})}
is a reversible transformation. In a (weakly) causal theory
we require
\mathcal{R}
to be just left-reversible.

Indeed, if a test is coarse-grained to a reversible transformation, then
its effect can be corrected by inverting it. Restricting to the scenario of (weakly) causal theories, it is
reasonable
to relax the above definition including the possibility that the channel
\mathcal{R}
is just left-reversible, meaning
that the correction can be applied
after
the test. The above condition in the (weakly) causal case
states that a test is non-disturbing if its effect can be corrected not only on local states
\rho\in{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A})}
,
but also when it is locally applied to every state
\Psi\in{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A}{\rm C})}
for every ancillary system
{\rm C}
. This
criterion captures the meaning of disturbance also in theories without local tomography, where a test that preserves
all local states
\rho\in{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A})}
, still could modify a state
\Psi\in{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A}{\rm C})}
for some system
{\rm C}
.
An example of this phenomenon occurs in fermionic quantum theory
Bravyi and Kitaev 2002
;
D’Ariano et al. 2014a
;
D’Ariano et al. 2014b
as
shown in Ref.
D’Ariano et al. 2020a
.

A central role in the above classification is played by the identity transformation as established by the following proposition, that summarises various results proved in Ref.
D’Ariano et al. 2020a

Proposition 6

(Atomic decomposition of the identity)
.

Given an operational probabilistic theory one of the following holds:

1.

The identity transformation is atomic for every system.
This is a necessary and sufficient condition for no-information without disturbance.

2.

The identity is not atomic for some system. The non redundant atomic refinement
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A})
of the identity

\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}=\sum_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}
is unique with
\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{A}_{j}=\mathcal{A}_{i}\delta_{ij}
. Thus, the sets of states and effects of system
{\rm A}
decompose as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} {\mathsf{St}}({\rm A})&=\bigoplus_{i\in{\rm X}}{\mathsf{St}}_{i}({\rm A}),\\
{\mathsf{Eff}}({\rm A})&=\bigoplus_{i\in{\rm X}}{\mathsf{Eff}}_{i}({\rm A}),\end{aligned}

(23)

where
{\mathsf{St}}_{i}({\rm A})\coloneqq\mathcal{A}_{i}{\mathsf{St}}({\rm A})
and
{\mathsf{Eff}}_{i}({\rm A})\coloneqq{\mathsf{Eff}}({\rm A})\mathcal{A}_{i}
.
Moreover, for any pair of systems
{\rm A}
,
{\rm B}
, with non redundant atomic decompositions
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
,
\{\mathcal{B}_{j}\}_{j\in{\rm Y}}
of the identities
\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\rm B}
, one
has the following decomposition of the set of states and of the set of effects of
{\rm A}{\rm B}

\displaystyle{\mathsf{St}}({\rm A}{\rm B})

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{(i,j)\in{\rm X}\times{\rm Y}}\bigoplus_{k\in K(i,j)}{\mathsf{St}}_{ijk}({\rm A}{\rm B}),

(24)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Eff}}({\rm A}{\rm B})

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{(i,j)\in{\rm X}\times{\rm Y}}\bigoplus_{k\in K(i,j)}{\mathsf{Eff}}_{ijk}({\rm A}{\rm B}),

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{j}{\mathsf{St}}({\rm A}{\rm B})

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{k\in K(i,j)}{\mathsf{St}}_{ijk}({\rm A}{\rm B}),

(25)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Eff}}({\rm A}{\rm B})\mathcal{A}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{j}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{k\in K(i,j)}{\mathsf{Eff}}_{ijk}({\rm A}{\rm B}).

Clearly,

\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\Psi_{i^{\prime}}\right)=\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\left|\Psi_{i}\right)
, and
\left(c_{j^{\prime}}\right|\mathcal{A}_{j}=\delta_{jj^{\prime}}\left(c_{j}\right|

for all
\Psi_{i^{\prime}}\in{\mathsf{St}}_{i^{\prime}}({\rm A})
and

c_{j^{\prime}}\in{\mathsf{Eff}}_{j^{\prime}}({\rm A})
.

In case
2
there is always some information that can be extracted without disturbance—from Proposition
6
, this is clearly the
information corresponding to the outcomes of tests refining the identity transformation. As a special case one has the
class of theories with full-information without disturbance (case
(iii)
), where any test can be realized in such
a way that via a post-processing its effects can be “erased”.

Definition 10

(Full-information without disturbance)
.

A theory satisfies full-information without disturbance if for every

\mathsf{A}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B})}
one has
\mathsf{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}
.

IV.2
Necessary and sufficient conditions for full-compatibility

We are now interested in characterizing the class of theories that do not include incompatible tests. We then introduce the class of fully-compatible theories as the class of theories satisfying the following feature:

Definition 11

(Full-compatibility)
.

A theory satisfies full-compatibility if for every pair of tests

\mathsf{A}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B})}
and
\mathsf{B}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm C})}
,
one has
\mathsf{A}\leftrightarrow\mathsf{B}
.

We can now prove equivalent conditions for full-compatibility. We show that the class of theories satisfying full
compatibility coincides with the class of theories with full-information without disturbance. Moreover, we show that
full compatibility is equivalent to the condition that any test can be simulated using a non-disturbing test, possibly involving an ancillary system.
Before proving the main result we present a preliminary lemma.

Lemma 3
.

In any theory violating no-information without disturbance there exist systems with an arbitrarily large number of perfectly discriminable states.

Proof.

According to Proposition
6
in any theory where no-information without
disturbance does not hold there exists a system
{\rm A}
such that the identity
\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}
is not atomic.
Let
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
, with
|{\rm X}|=m\geq 2
, be the unique non redundant atomic decomposition of
\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}
.
Therefore,
again by Proposition
6
,
\{\mathcal{A}_{i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n}}\}_{(i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n})\in{\rm X}^{n}}
, with
\mathcal{A}_{i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n}}\coloneqq\mathcal{A}_{i_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{i_{2}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathcal{A}_{i_{n}}
,
is a decomposition of the identity of system
{\rm N}\coloneqq{\rm A}^{\otimes n}
, and the set of states
{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm N})}
splits in
mn
blocks, i.e.
{\mathsf{St}}({\rm N})=\bigoplus_{(i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n})\in{\rm X}^{n}}{\mathsf{St}}_{i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n}}{({\rm N})}
—where
{\mathsf{St}}_{i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n}}{({\rm N})}
in turn splits as
{\mathsf{St}}_{i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n}}{({\rm N})}=\bigoplus_{k\in K(i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n})}{\mathsf{St}}_{i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{N}k}({\rm N})
. Since each block spans at least a one-dimensional vector space, there exists a set of non-null states

\{\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n}}\}_{(i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n})\in{\rm X}^{n}}
such that
\mathcal{A}_{i^{\prime}_{1}i^{\prime}_{2}\ldots i^{\prime}_{n}}\left|\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n}}\right)=\delta_{i_{1}i^{\prime}_{1}}\delta_{i_{2}i^{\prime}_{2}}\ldots\delta_{i_{n}i^{\prime}_{n}}\left|\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n}}\right)
. Moreover, since the theory is strongly causal and each state is proportional to a deterministic one, one can always consider the set of deterministic states
\{\rho_{i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n}}\}_{(i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n})\in{\rm X}^{n}}
, where
\left|\rho_{i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n}}\right)=\left|\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n}}\right)/(e\left|\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n}}\right)
, where
e

denotes the deterministic effect of system
{\rm N}
. The last set still satisfies the relation
\mathcal{A}_{i^{\prime}_{1}i^{\prime}_{2}\ldots i^{\prime}_{n}}\left|\rho_{i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n}}\right)=\delta_{i_{1}i^{\prime}_{1}}\delta_{i_{2}i^{\prime}_{2}}\ldots\delta_{i_{n}i^{\prime}_{n}}\left|\rho_{i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n}}\right)
. Finally, we observe that the observation test

\{a_{i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n}}\}_{(i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n})\in{\rm X}^{n}}
, with
\left(a_{i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n}}\right|\coloneqq\left(e\right|\mathcal{A}_{i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n}}
, is such that

(a_{i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n}}|\rho_{i^{\prime}_{1}i^{\prime}_{2}\ldots i^{\prime}_{n}})=\delta_{i_{1}i^{\prime}_{1}}\delta_{i_{2}i^{\prime}_{2}}\ldots\delta_{i_{n}i^{\prime}_{n}}
.

\,\blacksquare

We are now ready to prove the main result in this section.

Theorem 1
.

The following conditions are equivalent

1.

The theory satisfies full compatibility

2.

The theory satisfies full information without disturbance

3.

For every test

\mathsf{A}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B})}
one has

Proof.

.

(32)

with
\mathcal{Z}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm B}\tilde{\rm B},{\rm A})}
the left-inverse of
\mathcal{V}
, as post-processing map and sum over
(k,l)\in{\rm Z}\times{\rm L}_{j}
. Analogously one can show that also
\mathsf{B}\rightarrow\mathsf{A}
holds.

\,\blacksquare

Corollary 2
.

A theory has some information that cannot be extracted without disturbance if and only if
there exist incompatible tests.

Proof.

This trivially follows from the equivalence between full-compatibility and full-information without disturbance.

\,\blacksquare

We conclude this Section proving that in a theory without incompatibility all the systems must be classical according to the following definition

Definition 12

(Classical systmes)
.

A system
{\rm A}
, with linear dimension
d=\dim{({\mathsf{St}}_{\mathbb{R}}({\rm A}))}=\dim{({\mathsf{Eff}}_{\mathbb{R}}({\rm A}))}
, is classical if
the unique atomic non-redundant refinement of the identity
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i{\rm X}}
has cardinality
|{\rm X}|=d
and each block in the decomposition of Eq. (
23
) is one dimensional, namely
\dim(\mathcal{A}_{i}{\mathsf{St}}_{\mathbb{R}}({\rm A}))=\dim(\mathcal{A}_{i}{\mathsf{Eff}}_{\mathbb{R}}({\rm A}))=1
for every
i\in{\rm X}
.

We observe that according to the above definition a system is classical if and only if all its pure states are jointly
perfectly discriminable. In this case the base of the conic hull of the set of states of each system is a simplex,
which corresponds to a subset of the set of states for a convex theory. For a (weakly) causal theory, the set of
states is a simplex itself, with
d+1
vertices represented by the null state
0\in{\mathsf{St}}({\rm A})
and the
d

deterministic pure states. An example of theory with such systems is obviously the customary classical information
theory. However, even a theory where all systems are classical could differ from classical
information theory, in particular in the rule for parallel composition of systems (see Ref.
D’Ariano et al. 2020b

for an example).

Lemma 4
.

Let
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A})}
and
\{\mathcal{B}_{j}\}_{j\in{\rm Y}}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A})}
be the unique non-redundant atomic decomposition of
\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\rm B}
, respectively. If
\mathcal{C}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B})}
is atomic then one has
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{B}_{j}
for some
i\in{\rm X}
and
j\in{\rm Y}
.

Proof.

Since
\sum_{i,j}\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{B}_{j}=\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}
is atomic one has
\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{B}_{j}=\lambda_{ij}\mathcal{C}
, with
\lambda_{ij}\geq 0
, for every
i\in{\rm X}
and
j\in{\rm Y}
. On the other hand it is
\mathcal{A}_{k}\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{B}_{j}\mathcal{B}_{l}=\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{B}_{j}
showing that
\lambda_{ij}=1
for one choice of
i,j
while it is null otherwise.

\,\blacksquare

Proposition 7
.

In a theory with full compatibility all system of the theory are classical.

Proof.

Consider an atomic effect
a\in{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm A})}
. If
\{\mathcal{B}_{j}\}_{j\in{\rm Y}}
is the unique non-redundant decomposition of the identity of system
{\rm A}
, then it must be

\left(a\right|\mathcal{B}_{\bar{j}}=\left(a\right|
for some
{\bar{j}}\in{\rm Y}
. Let
\mathcal{A}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B})}
be an atomic transformation such that
\left(e\right|\mathcal{A}=\mu\left(a\right|
, with
e
the deterministic effect of system
{\rm A}
and
1\geq\mu>0
. Since
\mathcal{A}
is atomic, due to Lemma
4
, it must be
\left(e\right|\mathcal{A}=\left(e\right|\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}_{\bar{j}}
.
Consider now a test
\mathsf{A}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
such that
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{\bar{i}}
for some
\bar{i}\in{\rm X}
. Since by hypothesis all tests are weakly compatible, it is
\mathsf{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}
, and using Definition
7
one has

(35)

and using Eq. (
34
), remembering that channels preserve the deterministic effect, one finally gets

\left(a\right|\propto\left(e\right|\mathcal{B}_{\bar{j}}
.
Since this argument holds for all atomic effects in
{\mathsf{Eff}}_{\bar{j}}{({\rm A})}
, we conclude that

{\mathsf{Eff}}_{\bar{j}}{({\rm A})}
has linear dimension equal to one.

\,\blacksquare

V
Discussion

We have introduced two alternative definitions of compatible tests of a causal operational probabilistic theory. We named the two conditions strong and weak compatibility because there exist weakly compatible tests that are not strongly compatible, e. g. any pair of reversible quantum channels. For the case of channels, strong compatibility coincides with the notion of channels compatibility already given in Refs.
Heinosaari et al. 2016
;
Heinosaari and Miyadera 2017
. We have then shown that for the case of observables strong and weak compatibility coincide. Finally we proved that a necessary and sufficient condition for full-compatibility, namely all tests of the theory are weakly compatible, is full-information without disturbance, namely all the information can be extracted without disturbance. Moreover, both conditions imply that all the systems of the theory are classical systems.

On the basis of our results we understand that generalizing the notion of measurement incompatibility to arbitrary devices, say instruments, is not straightforward. If for two measurements the ability of implementing them simultaneously (strong compatibility) or in a sequence (weak compatibility) is equivalent, for two instruments it turns out that it is not. This opens the problem of characterizing the two different kinds of incompatibility in terms of resources for quantum information tasks. Despite our results hold for arbitrary causal theories, the discrepancy between weak and strong compatibility already occurs for quantum channels, and a first analysis can focus on the quantum case. Recently, quantum measurement incompatibility, which is known to be a necessary condition for violating Bell inequalities and for steering, has been characterized in terms of quantum programmability of instruments, namely the temporal freedom of a user in issuing programs to a quantum device
Buscemi et al. 2020
. It is reasonable to assume that one of the two notions of quantum instruments incompatibility should be the analogue, in terms of resources for processing of programmable instruments, to that of measurements.

Finally, as quantum channels are all weakly compatible, it is clear that the source of weak incompatibility for
quantum instruments resides in the irreversible process of information extraction, which cannot be reduced to a
reversible interaction with the detector by any means. Indeed, if this were the case, the two notions of strong
and weak compatibility would coincide in the quantum case. On the other hand, it is pretty well understood that
strong incompatibility for quantum instruments is connected with the impossibility of cloning.
